Perfect runs100%runs where every grader passed
How to read this report
  • A run's score is the weighted fraction of its graders that passed; a "perfect" run passed every grader.
  • A case's score is the mean of its runs; a case passes when its score is at or above the 100% threshold (the tick on each case bar).
  • The suite score is the mean of the per-case scores.
  • Judge votes are independent samples of the LLM judge; the majority decides pass or fail.
  • "With plugin" and "Baseline" runs are identical except for the plugin being loaded; Δ is the with-plugin score minus the baseline score.

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: Inventory implicit design-system components in this monorepo (apps + packages/ui). Read-only; no working tree changes. Report inventory, top few consolidation wins, and exact coverage (what was/wasn't inspected). Not a massive refactor plan."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"7e51bf3c-96f7-46e1-9571-5c06b6b223a5","uuid":"62364bac-a4d6-465e-8c61-79c9e4e2c253","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:01:40.376Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: Monorepo with apps and a UI kit. Read-only inventory, top few consolidation wins, explicit coverage report. Do not modify the working tree. Starting inputs: AGENTS.md, docs/DESIGN.md, packages/ui/README.md, packages/ui/src/components/Card.tsx, apps/player/Home.tsx, apps/admin/Users.tsx"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"eb0a063a-43d5-4cd7-9127-f7a669b29fdc","uuid":"285241ef-cecd-4422-8db6-bdc582ecd431","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:02:12.705Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"d41042d1-41c2-4a3f-8308-1325c6eb536b","uuid":"571da9d7-3f57-4f4e-b606-8fa155255408","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:02:46.805Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"fab148d9-93e7-4f0b-a578-ff2fc12c2f25","uuid":"8e06f44a-4d46-41eb-b176-8952493eb841","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:04:35.208Z","isSynthetic":true}

Neither instance has `role=\"status\"` or `role=\"alert\"` or an icon, so the warning is shown only through colour and the left border. The current class has no focus, hover or disabled styles, and it relies on the browser's default border. Fixing those in one shared place pays off right away.\n   - **Smallest step:** Wrap the existing class, keep the API to `variant` plus the normal button props, and add a `:focus-visible` style.\n   - **Confidence:** Medium. The contract is clear, but there are only two uses.\n\n3. **Panel → `<Panel title>`**\n   - **Why:** It repeats cleanly, but it's purely visual. A shared CSS class already gives you most of the consistency.\n   - **Smallest step:** Either keep `.panel` as a documented recipe or make a thin component that takes a `title`. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: Find the unofficial UI kit: inspect README.md, src/pages/billing.tsx, src/pages/team.tsx, src/styles.css. Show repeated patterns with examples and suggest what's worth standardizing. Read-only."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"057379eb-e5d2-4ba0-af41-1228f68d46d9","uuid":"f23149d0-5b45-4308-a4b8-73d69458a242","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:05:06.544Z","isSynthetic":true}

t | `styles.css:3`; `billing.tsx:1` (\"Card expires soon\"); `team.tsx:1` (\"One invite is expiring\") | Confirmed: 2 uses. Same `.notice` + modifier setup, with only `warning` defined |\n| **Page layout** | `main` → `h1` → panel → notice below it | `billing.tsx:1`, `team.tsx:1` | Confirmed: the structure is identical. This is a page template, not a primitive |\n| **Invoice preview** | `.billing-invoice-preview` (monospace) | `styles.css:4` only | Meant to be a one-off (README says so). **Neither page uses this class.** It may be dead CSS, or it's used in code that isn't in this repo |\n\n## What's worth standardizing (in priority order)\n\n1. **Warning notice → a small `<Notice tone=\"warning\">` component.** Confidence: high.\n   - **Evidence:** 2 identical uses, and both are status messages users will see.\n   - **Why:** this is the one with a real behavior gap. Neither instance has `role=\"status\"` or `role=\"alert\"`, so screen readers don't announce it. Fixing that in one component fixes it everywhere it's used later. The `.notice` + `.warning` naming already points toward a `tone` prop.\n   - **Smallest step:** a component that sets the class and an ARIA role. Don't add info/error/success tones until a screen actually needs one.\n\n2. **Primary action → `<Button variant=\"primary\">`, or at least a written rule for the class.** Confidence: medium-high.\n   - **Evidence:** 2 uses.\n   - **Why:** neither `<button>` sets `type`. Inside a form it would default to `submit`, which is an easy bug. The CSS also has no hover, focus or disabled styles, so a shared component is the one place to add those. Right now `.action` is an empty base class.\n   - **Smallest step:** a thin wrapper that defaults `type=\"button\"` and keeps the existing class names.\n\n3. **Pull the raw values into CSS variables.** Confidence: medium.\n   - **Values:** `#1769aa`, `#d97706`, `#fff4d6`, `#ddd`, plus radii 12px/999px and spacing 24/12/10/18px.\n   - **Why:** each value appears only once today, so it isn't urgent. It's cheap, though, and it's what you'd need for theming or a second accent color.\n   - **Smallest step:** a few `:root` custom properties in `styles.css`. Hold off on a full token system.\n\n4. **Panel → `<Panel title=…>`.** Confidence: medium.\n   - **Why:** it's repeated, but it's only a styled `div`, and the class works fine as is. A component would mainly fix the heading level (always `h2`) and the heading-then-action layout. Do it if you're building the other two components anyway. Otherwise the class is enough.\n\n5. **Leave these alone for now:**\n   - **Page layout:** 2 instances isn't enough to justify a `<SettingsPage>` template.\n   - **Invoice preview:** it's intentionally local. Check whether `.billing-invoice-preview` is still used anywhere and delete it if not.\n\n## What I inspected and what I couldn't check\n- **Inspected:** every file in the repo: `README.md`, `src/styles.css`, `src/pages/billing.tsx` and `src/pages/team.tsx`. Within this repo, this list is complete.\n- **Not available:** no `package.json`, no entry point and no router, so I can't tell how `styles.css` is loaded or whether other code uses these classes.\n- **Not done:** I only read the source. I didn't render anything or check visuals. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"bee3047c-ae1c-476f-a0b4-7fc88022c07b","uuid":"4a7aa1c2-ac60-4a6d-abba-e191fd0fc49a","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:05:39.865Z","isSynthetic":true}

 |\n| **Warning notice** | `div.notice.warning`: 4px amber (`#d97706`) left border, 12px padding, `#fff4d6` background, plain text. Same two-class setup: `.notice` has no styles and no other tones exist. | `styles.css:3`; `billing.tsx:1` (\"Card expires soon\"); `team.tsx:1` (\"One invite is expiring\") | Missing primitive. Don't standardize it. |\n\n## What's worth standardizing (ranked)\n\n1. **The primary button.** The visual style is already consistent, so the value is in the gaps, not in deduplication. There are no hover, focus-visible, or disabled styles, and neither `<button>` sets `type`. A small `Button` (or a `.action` base class with a `variant` modifier) is where those states would live once for every page. **Smallest step:** move the base styles into `.action`, keep `.primary` as a modifier, and add focus and disabled states. A React wrapper is optional. **Confidence: medium-high.**\n\n2. **The warning notice.** The `.notice` + `.warning` naming already implies tones, but there's only one. The bigger issue is accessibility: the notices have no `role`, so screen readers may not announce messages like \"Card expires soon\". **Smallest step:** a `Notice tone=\"warning\"` component, or a documented recipe, that sets a suitable `role` (`status` for passive messages). Add new tones only when a real second one shows up. **Confidence: medium.**\n\n3. **Colors and spacing as variables.** Four colors, two radii, and several padding values (24, 12, 10/18 px) are hardcoded, with no scale. A handful of CSS custom properties (`--color-primary`, `--color-warning`, `--color-warning-bg`, `--color-border`, `--radius-card`, `--space-*`) would stop new screens from picking slightly different values. **Confidence: medium.** It's cheap, but with three rules the drift risk is still low.\n\n4. **Panel: keep it lightweight.** The panel-with-heading-plus-action layout repeats, but the CSS is one line and works fine as a class. Only make it a `Panel title=… actions=…` component if more screens adopt the same heading-then-action layout. For now, the class is enough. **Confidence: low-medium.**\n\n**Don't standardize:** the invoice preview, which is intentionally one-off and unused in these pages, or the page layout, which is two instances with no variation to capture.\n\n**Caveat:** each pattern appears exactly twice. That's enough to show they're shared, but not enough to know which variants you'll need. I'd keep the APIs as small as above and not build a component library yet.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\n- **Inspected:** every file in the repo: `README.md`, `src/styles.css`, `src/pages/billing.tsx`, `src/pages/team.tsx`. Both uses pair it with an `h2`, so building the heading in keeps the heading level consistent.\n2. **`Button`** with `variant=\"primary\"`: it already has a name and a variant scheme. I'd only add the variants that actually exist (just `primary`) and not invent `secondary` or `danger` ahead of need.\n3. **`Notice`** with `tone=\"warning\"`: same reasoning. It's also a good place to add `role=\"status\"` once, which neither page has now. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"8ee52b5d-2883-4180-9133-23e015e2390c","uuid":"7352c204-723b-424b-9b53-190e3e764dd2","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:07:21.967Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"a85b8cb3-2bf9-42b3-8288-e45facf84471","uuid":"dab6ade6-c5ad-4af0-978a-a24e833969b6","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:07:54.393Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /home/juan/dotfiles/skills/design-system-audit\n\n# Design System Audit\n\nDiscover the design system the codebase already implements, including patterns that exist only as repeated markup, utility classes, local CSS, or conventions. The goal is an evidence-backed map of reusable UI contracts—not a component wishlist or a visual redesign.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Default to a **read-only audit**. Never edit source, create backlog items, or make visual changes unless requested.\n- Inspect and preserve the working tree. Note relevant pre-existing changes; do not attribute them to the audit or overwrite them.\n- Follow repository instructions and read its design docs, token sources, and shared-component guidance first. Treat code and runtime behavior as evidence; documentation describes intent and can be stale.\n- Separate **explicit primitives** (named reusable exports) from **implicit patterns** (repeated or standardized compositions embedded in screens). A single domain-specific component is not automatically a system primitive.\n- Infer repetition using structure, styling, states, and behavior—not class-name similarity alone. Group variants when they share a stable contract; keep domain compositions distinct when their meaning or behavior differs.\n- Cite paths and useful line ranges when available. Support claims with multiple instances for a repeated pattern. Label tentative inferences and conflicts between docs and implementation.\n- Do not claim “all” from a keyword search. Explain the surfaces and source areas actually inspected, and list meaningful exclusions.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. **Set scope.** Identify framework, app/package boundaries, entrypoints, and local instructions. Inspect `git status` before exploring; avoid writing.\n2. **Read the declared system.** Find design-system docs, tokens, component packages, exports, catalogs, and adoption/drift checks. Establish intended ownership and naming.\n3. **Map explicit components.** Inventory shared primitives by family: actions, fields, selection, overlays, feedback, data display, navigation/layout, identity, and loading/error/empty states. Include relevant utilities and behavior primitives when they define a visual contract.\n4. **Discover implicit patterns.** Search app screens, local components, scoped/global CSS, and repeated utility recipes. Compare candidate instances for structural and behavioral contracts. Typical leads include cards/panels, buttons, banners/alerts, form groups, tabs, menus/pickers, dialogs, tables, badges, stat blocks, navigation, and skeletons. These are search leads, not a required taxonomy.\n5. **Compare against authority.** Identify local twins of shared primitives, missing shared patterns, token/shape/type/theme drift, and intentional domain-specific compositions. Verify examples in source rather than relying on naming or docs alone.\n6. **Prioritize recommendations.** Rank by repetition, user-visible inconsistency, behavior/accessibility risk, and blast radius. Prefer the smallest useful shared API or recipe; do not recommend abstraction when evidence is weak. Keep recommendations separate from findings.\n7. **Report honest coverage.** State apps/packages/files or representative routes inspected, method, notable exclusions, and whether the inventory is exhaustive or scoped. For large repos, offer a first-pass map without presenting it as complete.\n\n## Output format\n\n# Implicit Design-System Audit\n\n## Summary\nBriefly state the existing explicit foundation and the main implicit-system finding.\n\n## Explicit foundation\nList authoritative token sources, shared primitive packages, docs/catalogs, and relevant enforcement/adoption mechanisms.\n\n## Implicit component inventory\nUse a compact table with: **pattern**, **repeated contract/variants**, **evidence** (concrete source paths), and **relationship to shared primitive** (local twin, missing primitive, domain composition, or uncertain).\nGroup related instances instead of listing every element individually. Clearly distinguish confirmed repetition from a plausible pattern requiring more review.\n\n## Prioritized consolidation opportunities\nRank the few highest-value opportunities. For each: evidence, why it merits consolidation (or why it may remain local), smallest useful next step, and confidence. Do not produce implementation changes by default.\n\n## Coverage and limitations\nEnumerate inspected app/package surfaces and exclusions; disclose dirty-worktree caveats and incomplete census limits. Never imply runtime or visual verification if only source was inspected.\n\n## Quality bar\nA useful audit answers: “What is already a component in practice?”, “Where is its contract encoded?”, “What shared primitive already owns it?”, and “What is worth consolidating next?” Avoid generic inventories with no evidence, false duplication based on superficial CSS matches, and unbounded recommendations to build a design system.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"e7d1654b-200e-4743-815e-3f1d997992b7","uuid":"cffc99c3-eb9a-4ae7-9acc-4258b2b1a031","timestamp":"2026-11-11T22:08:34.589Z","isSynthetic":true}

ng `Card`.\n\n**2. The contract also says to use `@acme/ui` for buttons, so this is a direct breach of it.\n\n**3. A \"titled panel with actions\" pattern: 2 copies**\nBoth screens use the same structure: a card section, then an `<h2>` title, then one primary action. This is the real component hiding in these screens, something like a page or section header. It's **not** in the shared package's export list. Two copies is enough to name it, but I'd create it as an admin-local component first rather than promote it to `@acme/ui` straight away.\n\n## Should stay domain-specific (or is undecided)\n\n- **The audit table** (`audit.tsx:1`, `<table className=\"audit-grid\">`). `docs/design.md:2` explicitly lets \"dense data tables… stay local when behavior differs.\" The code doesn't show any different behavior, though: it's a static header row (Event, Actor) with no `thead`/`tbody`, sorting, density handling or virtualization. **I can't tell from the source whether it should become `DataTable`.** That depends on what the audit log needs to do and on `DataTable`'s API, which I couldn't see. Ask the audit-screen owner before migrating it.\n- **The \"No users yet\" empty state** (`users.tsx:1`). It's currently styled as a card. Wrapping it in `Card` is a safe step. A dedicated empty-state component would be premature with only one example.\n- **Domain wording and actions** (\"Invite\", \"Export\", and later \"workflow-specific status panels\" per the design doc) should stay in the routes. Only the containers around them should be shared.\n\n## What I'd consolidate first: Button\n\nIt's the one shared component whose source actually exists, it's explicitly required by the contract, and it's an exact duplicate in both screens.\n\nThere's one blocker. As written, `packages/ui/src/Button.tsx:1` only accepts `variant` and `children`. It doesn't pass through `onClick`, `type`, `disabled` or `aria-*`. The local buttons have no handlers yet either, but Invite and Export will need them. If you migrate as-is, people will go back to raw `<button>` the first time they need a click handler. So the order is:\n\n1. Make `Button` forward `...rest` (and set `type=\"button\"` by default), then confirm the `primary` variant matches `bg-blue-600` in `tokens.css`. That file is missing here, so I couldn't check.\n2. Replace the 2 local buttons with `<Button variant=\"primary\">`.\n3. Replace the 3 panel copies with `Card`, after confirming `Card` actually exists in the real package.\n4. Extract the title-plus-action header as an admin-local component. Promote it to `@acme/ui` only if a third app or screen needs it.\n5. Decide the audit table vs `DataTable` question with the domain owner. Don't migrate it automatically.\n\n**Before acting on any of this, check the README against the real package.** Five of the six exports it lists, plus the tokens file, aren't in this checkout. This may be a trimmed fixture, but if the real package looks the same, the docs are ahead of the code.
